Efficient Algorithms for Recognizing Weighted Tree-Adjoining Languages
Alexandra Butoi, Tim Vieira, Ryan Cotterell David Chiang, [1ex] ETH Zürich

Abstract
The class of tree-adjoining languages can be characterized by various two-level formalisms, consisting of a context-free grammar (CFG) or pushdown automaton (PDA) controlling another CFG or PDA. These four formalisms are equivalent to tree-adjoining grammars (TAG), linear indexed grammars (LIG), pushdown-adjoining automata (PAA), and embedded pushdown automata (EPDA). We define semiring-weighted versions of the above two-level formalisms, and we design new algorithms for computing their stringsums (the weight of all derivations of a string) and allsums (the weight of all derivations). From these, we also immediately obtain stringsum and allsum algorithms for TAG, LIG, PAA, and EPDA. For LIG, our algorithm is more time-efficient by a factor of 𝒪 ⁡ ( n ​ | 𝒩 | ) \mathcal{O}(n|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}|) (where n n is the string length and | 𝒩 | |{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}| is the size of the nonterminal set) and more space-efficient by a factor of 𝒪 ⁡ ( | Γ | ) \mathcal{O}(|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}|) (where Γ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma} is the size of the stack alphabet) than the algorithm of Vijay-Shanker and Weir 1989 . For EPDA, our algorithm is both more space-efficient and time-efficient than the algorithm of Alonso et al. 2001 by factors of 𝒪 ⁡ ( | Γ | 2 ) \mathcal{O}(|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}|^{2}) and 𝒪 ⁡ ( | Γ | 3 ) \mathcal{O}(|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}|^{3}) , respectively. Finally, we give the first PAA stringsum and allsum algorithms.



1
Introduction

Weir 1992
introduced a hierarchy of formal languages whose first level (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{L}_{1}}{}
) is the class of context-free languages and second level (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{L}_{2}}{}
) is the class of tree-adjoining languages.
Just as context-free languages can be characterized by both context-free grammars and pushdown automata, tree-adjoining languages are characterized by multiple formalisms as well, including tree-adjoining grammars
(
Joshi et al. 1975
, TAG;)
, linear indexed grammars
(
Gazdar 1988
, LIG;)
, embedded pushdown automata
(
Vijay-Shanker 1987
, EPDA;)
, and pushdown-adjoining automata
(
Butoi et al. 2023
, PAA;)
.

Tree-adjoining languages can further be characterized through the mechanism of
control

(
Weir 1992
)
, which yields various
two-level
formalisms. Specifically, we have shown that CFGs controlled by CFGs, CFGs controlled by PDAs, PDAs controlled by CFGs, and PDAs controlled by PDAs are equivalent to TAG, LIG, PAA, and EPDA, respectively, in a strict sense called
d-strong
equivalence
(
Butoi et al. 2023
)
.

Figure 1:
Overview of the results presented in this paper. Solid lines are new results shown in the paper; dashed lines are old results. An arrow
X\rightarrow Y
means “
X
can be converted to
Y
”; an arrow
X\twoheadrightarrow Y
means “
X
has an algorithm for computing
Y
". We only show the conversions and algorithms for CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG; the results for the other three two-level formalisms are analogous.

When designing statistical parsers for tree-adjoining formalisms, it is often useful to consider their semiring-weighted generalizations. In this paper, we introduce semiring-weighted versions of the above two-level formalisms, and we give new, more efficient algorithms for computing stringsums (the total weight of all derivations of a string) in these formalisms.

Lang 1974
gives a recognition algorithm for (what we call) simple PDAs, which can pop and push at most one stack symbol.
We have shown that this algorithm is suboptimal, and that (what we call) top-down PDAs, which always pop exactly one stack symbol, allow computing stringsums more efficiently
(
Butoi et al. 2022
)
.

Existing definitions of LIG
Vijay-Shanker and Weir 1989
;
Joshi et al. 1991
;
Kanazawa 2014
are equivalent to CFGs controlled by simple PDAs, and existing recognition algorithms
(
Vijay-Shanker and Weir 1989
, e.g.,)
have the same limitation that makes Lang’s algorithm suboptimal. By using a top-down PDA as a controller instead, we obtain a new stringsum algorithm that is more space-efficient and runs asymptotically faster. Additionally, we obtain an algorithm for allsums (the total weight of all derivations) that is more space-efficient.

All stringsum algorithms that operate directly on EPDAs that we are aware of
(
Alonso et al. 2000
;
Alonso et al. 2001
)
are designed for specific types of EPDAs, failing to take advantage of the structure of computation in top-down PDAs.
We design a new stringsum algorithm for a subclass of EPDA that is equivalent to a top-down PDA controlled by a top-down PDA, and we obtain both time and space improvements over these previous algorithms.
Additionally, we design a more space-efficient EPDA allsum algorithm.

Our algorithms assume that CFGs are given in Chomsky normal form, and for PDAs, we define a new normal form that is exactly analogous to the Chomsky normal form.
We show that applying these normal forms to controllers and controllees induces, for free, normal forms for the two-level formalisms and for TAG, LIG, PAA, and EPDA.
The conversions into the normal forms for all these formalisms become
simpler than direct conversions, as they only require
CFG or PDA conversions.
We leave extensions of our stringsum and allsum algorithms to general CFGs/PDAs for future work.

The main contributions of this paper are:

•

Semiring-weighted versions of the formalisms CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG (
section

3
), PDA
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG, CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA, and PDA
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA (
section

B.1
).

•

Normal forms for these formalisms that arise, for free, from the normal forms of the controller/controllee CFGs/PDAs (
sections

3.2
and
B.2
).

•

Stringsum algorithms for
CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG (
section

4
), PDA
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG, and PDA
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA (
section

C.2
) that are more time-efficient or space-efficient than the existing algorithms for their equivalent LIG and EPDA.

•

The first stringsum algorithm for CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA, and therefore, for PAA (
section

C.2
).

•

Algorithms for computing allsums in
these two-level formalisms (
sections

5
and
D
).

2
Preliminaries

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[i\mathord{:}j]}
denote the sequence of integers
(i,\ldots,j)
.
If
\boldsymbol{s}
is a string, we write
|\boldsymbol{s}|
for the length of
\boldsymbol{s}
,
\boldsymbol{s}_{i}
for the
i^{\text{th}}
symbol of
\boldsymbol{s}
, and
\boldsymbol{s}_{(i\mathord{:}j]}
for the substring
\boldsymbol{s}_{i+1}\cdots\boldsymbol{s}_{j}
.

2.1
Semiring-Weighted Languages

Throughout this paper, we assume that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{W}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W},\oplus,\otimes,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathbf{0}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\mathbf{1}}}}\right)}
is a commutative semiring.
We also sometimes assume that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{W}}
is
\omega
-continuous, which makes it possible to take (countably) infinite sums. Please see
section

A.1
for definitions of these terms.
Readers not familiar with semirings may safely assume that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}=\mathbb{R}_{{}\geq 0}\cup\{\infty\}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathbf{0}}}=0
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\mathbf{1}}}}=1
, and
\oplus
and
\otimes
are the usual addition and multiplication operations, respectively.

Definition 1
.

An
alphabet

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}
is a non-empty finite set of
symbols
.
A
language
over
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}
is a subset of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}
’s
Kleene closure

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}^{*}}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}\bigcup_{n\geq 0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}^{n}
.
A
weighted language
over
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}
with weights from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{W}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W},\oplus,\otimes,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathbf{0}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\mathbf{1}}}}\right)}
is a mapping from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}^{*}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}
.

This paper considers
weighted
formalisms, where the weights are taken from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{W}}
. Rather than just producing or recognizing strings from a formal language, these formalisms define a weighting function over
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}^{*}}
. When
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{W}}
is the boolean semiring
(\{0,1\},\lor,\land,0,1)
, we recover the usual notion of a formal language defined as a set of strings.

2.2
Weighted Context-Free Grammars

Definition 2
.

A
weighted context-free grammar
(WCFG) over a semiring
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{W}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W},\oplus,\otimes,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathbf{0}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\mathbf{1}}}}\right)}
is a tuple
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{R}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{w}},\mathit{S})}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}
are finite sets of nonterminal symbols and terminal symbols, respectively,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{R}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}})^{*}}
is a finite set of productions,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{w}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{R}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}
is a production-weighting function,
and
\mathit{S}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}
is the start symbol.

We write a production
(\mathit{X},\boldsymbol{\alpha})\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{R}}
as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
, and
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{w}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\right)=w
, we write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}\xrightarrow{w}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
.

A WCFG produces strings by starting from the symbol
\mathit{S}
and repeatedly replacing the leftmost nonterminal
\mathit{X}
with the right-hand side of a production
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
until no more nonterminals are left.
In the following definitions, let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{R}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{w}},\mathit{S})}
be a WCFG.

Definition 3
.

If
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\mathit{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\boldsymbol{\gamma}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}
are sequences of terminals and nonterminals of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}
, where
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}^{*}}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}})^{*}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p}=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}\xrightarrow{w}\boldsymbol{\gamma}})
is a production of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}
, we write
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\xRightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}
to denote that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}

derives

\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}
in one step using production
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p}
.

Definition 4
.

A
partial derivation
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}
from
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}
to
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n}
is a sequence of steps

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}\xRightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p}_{1}}\cdots\xRightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p}_{n}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n}
.
We write
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}\xRightarrow{*}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n}
to assert that some partial derivation from
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}
to
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n}
exists, or to denote the set of all such partial derivations.
If
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}=\mathit{S}
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n}=\boldsymbol{s}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}^{*}}
, we call
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}
a
derivation
of
\boldsymbol{s}
, or that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}

derives

\boldsymbol{s}
.

The
weight
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}
is the product of its production weights,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{w}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{w}}(p_{i}).

We denote by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}},\boldsymbol{s})
the
set of all derivations in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}
of
\boldsymbol{s}
and by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}})
the set of all derivations in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}
.

Definition 5
.

The
stringsum

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{w}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}},\boldsymbol{s})
of a string
\boldsymbol{s}
under
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}
is the total weight of all derivations in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}
for
\boldsymbol{s}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{w}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}},\boldsymbol{s})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}\bigoplus_{\mathclap{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}},\boldsymbol{s})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{w}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}).

Definition 6
.

The
allsum

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{w}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}})
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}
is the total weight of all its derivations,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{w}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}\bigoplus_{\mathclap{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{w}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}).

3
Semiring-Weighted CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG

Weir 1992
defined a hierarchy of formal languages and showed that its second level, which is commonly known as the class of tree-adjoining languages, can be obtained through the mechanism of control, using a CFG (the controllee) whose derivations are controlled by another CFG (the controller).
In previous work
(
Butoi et al. 2023
)
, we extended this mechanism to PDAs, both as controllers and controllees, and obtained four distinct formalisms by mixing a controller CFG or PDA with a controllee CFG or PDA.
In this paper, we use
semiring-weighted
versions of these formalisms. We give a formal definition of a weighted CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG here (see
section

B.1
for the other three definitions).

We denote such a grammar by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{2}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}
is the controller and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{2}
is the controllee.
Additionally, we use symbols
\mathit{X},\mathit{Y},\mathit{Z},\ldots
and
\mathit{a},\mathit{b},\mathit{c},\ldots
for nonterminals and terminals of the controllee, and
\mathsfit A,\mathsfit B,\mathsfit C,\ldots
and
\mathsfit a,\mathsfit b,\mathsfit c,\ldots
for nonterminals and terminals of the controller.

3.1
Definition

A weighted labeled distinguished CFG (WLD-CFG) is simply a WCFG where each production has a label, and its right-hand side has one “distinguished” occurrence of a nonterminal symbol. These two extensions allow its derivations to be controlled by another formalism.

Definition 7
.

A
weighted labeled distinguished context-free grammar
(WLD-CFG) over a semiring
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{W}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W},\oplus,\otimes,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathbf{0}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\mathbf{1}}}}\right)}
is a tuple
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{R}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{w}},\mathit{S})
, where

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}
are finite sets of nonterminal symbols, terminal symbols, and labels, respectively,

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{R}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}\times\mathbb{N}\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma})^{*}}
is a finite set of productions,

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{w}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{R}}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}
is a production-weighting function, and

•

\mathit{S}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}
is the start symbol.

If
(\ell,i,A,\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{n})
is a production in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{R}}
, we must have either
i=0
, which we write as

\ell:A\rightarrow\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{n}

or
1\leq i\leq n
and
\beta_{i}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}
, which we write as

\ell:A\rightarrow\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{i-1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{471102}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta_{i}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{471102}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta_{i}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{403821}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta_{i}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{373347}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta_{i}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}\beta_{i+1}\cdots\beta_{n}.

Weir 1992
gave two definitions of a derivation in an LD-CFG (the controllee) controlled by another CFG (the controller).
In his first definition, the controllee nonterminals keep a record of the productions used, called a
control word
(a string in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}^{*}}
). At the end of the derivation, each control word is checked for membership in the language of the controller.
In his second definition, the controllee nonterminals run a controller derivation. When the controller generates a label
\ell\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}
, it causes the controllee to apply production
\ell
.
We use the latter definition, which allows one to think of the controller and the controllee as a single grammar, merging their productions into a single set of rules.

For any sets
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, we define
\mathcal{X}[\mathcal{Y}]=\{\mathit{X}[\mathit{Y}]\mid\mathit{X}\in\mathcal{X},\mathit{Y}\in\mathcal{Y}\}
, where
\mathit{X}[\mathit{Y}]
is just another way of writing the ordered pair of
\mathit{X}
and
\mathit{Y}
.
If
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\mathit{X}_{1}\cdots\mathit{X}_{k}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}^{*}}
is a sequence of nonterminals, we use the notation
\boldsymbol{\alpha}[\mathit{Y}]=\mathit{X}_{1}[\mathit{Y}]\cdots\mathit{X}_{k}[\mathit{Y}]
for any
\mathit{Y}\in\mathcal{Y}
.

To make the following definition more readable, we assume that the controller’s right-hand sides are either a single label or a string of nonterminals, and that the controllee right-hand sides are either a single terminal or a string of nonterminals with exactly one distinguished position. It would be possible, but more tedious, to write the definition for the general case.

Definition 8
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}
be a WCFG with nonterminals
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}_{1}
and terminals
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}
, called the
controller
, and let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{2}
be a WLD-CFG with nonterminals
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}_{2}
and labels
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}
, called the
controllee
. Then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{2}
is a rewriting system with the following rules:

•

If
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A\xrightarrow{w}\boldsymbol{\beta}})
is a production of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}
where
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}_{1}^{*}}
, then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{2}
has a rule
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}[\mathsfit A\mathinner{\cdot\cdot}]\xrightarrow{w}\mathit{X}[\boldsymbol{\beta}\mathinner{\cdot\cdot}]}
for each
\mathit{X}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}_{2}
.

•

If
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A\xrightarrow{w_{1}}\ell})
is a production of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}
and
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell\colon\mathit{X}\xrightarrow{w_{2}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{389719}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{389719}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{317782}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{194859}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2}})
is a production of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{2}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{2}
has a rule
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}[\mathsfit A\mathinner{\cdot\cdot}]\xrightarrow{w_{1}\otimes w_{2}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}[\mathsfit S]\,\mathit{Y}[\mathinner{\cdot\cdot}]\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2}[\mathsfit S]}
.

A derivation in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{2}
starts with
\mathit{S}[\mathsfit S]
.
If there is a rule
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p}=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}[\mathsfit A\mathinner{\cdot\cdot}]\xrightarrow{w}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}\mathit{Y}[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\mathinner{\cdot\cdot}]\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2}})
, then for any
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}^{*}}
,
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}_{2}^{*}}
, and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{3}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}_{2}^{*}}])^{*}}
, we write

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}{\color[rgb]{0.6406,0.0273,0.4531}\mathit{X}[\mathsfit A\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}]}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{3}\xRightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}{\color[rgb]{0.6406,0.0273,0.4531}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4688,0.582}\mathit{Y}[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}]}{\color[rgb]{0.6406,0.0273,0.4531}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{3}.

Similarly, if there is a rule
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p}=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}[\mathsfit A]\xrightarrow{w}\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
, then for any
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}^{*}}
, and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{3}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}_{2}^{*}}])^{*}}
, we write

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}{\color[rgb]{0.6406,0.0273,0.4531}\mathit{X}[\mathsfit A]}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{3}\xRightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}{\color[rgb]{0.6406,0.0273,0.4531}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{3}.

We write
\xRightarrow{*}
to denote a derivation with zero or more steps, as in
4
.
If
\mathit{S}[\mathsfit S]\xRightarrow{*}\boldsymbol{s}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}^{*}}
, we say that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{2}

derives

\boldsymbol{s}
.

Example 1
.

Consider the following
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{2}
that generates the language
\{\mathrm{a}^{n}\mathrm{b}^{n}\mathrm{c}^{n}\mathrm{d}^{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
The controller is a WCFG
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}
with the start symbol
\mathsf{S}_{1}
and the set of productions

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{R}}_{1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\mathbf{1}}}}}\mathsf{T}\mathsf{L}_{3}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\mathbf{1}}}}}\mathsf{L}_{1}\mathsf{T}\mathsf{L}_{2}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\mathbf{1}}}}}\varepsilon}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}_{i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\mathbf{1}}}}}\ell_{i}}\quad(i\in[1:3])\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\mathbf{1}}}}}\ell_{i}}\quad(i\in[4:7])\end{array}\right\}

and the controllee is a WLD-CFG
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{2}
with start symbol
\mathrm{S}_{2}
and the set of productions

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{R}}_{2}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell_{1}\colon\mathrm{S}_{2}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\mathbf{1}}}}}\mathrm{A}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{291273}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{291273}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{231562}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{196611}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{2}\mathrm{D}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell_{4}\colon\mathrm{A}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\mathbf{1}}}}}\mathrm{a}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell_{2}\colon\mathrm{S}_{2}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\mathbf{1}}}}}\mathrm{B}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{291273}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{291273}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{231562}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{196611}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{2}\mathrm{C}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell_{5}\colon\mathrm{B}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\mathbf{1}}}}}\mathrm{b}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell_{3}\colon\mathrm{S}_{2}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\mathbf{1}}}}}\varepsilon}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell_{6}\colon\mathrm{C}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\mathbf{1}}}}}\mathrm{c}}\\
&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell_{7}\colon\mathrm{D}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\mathbf{1}}}}}\mathrm{d}}\end{array}\right\}.

Below is a derivation of the string
\mathrm{aabbccdd}
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}_{2}[\mathsf{S}_{1}]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathrm{S}_{2}[\mathsf{T}\mathsf{L}_{3}]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathrm{S}_{2}[\mathsf{L}_{1}\mathsf{T}\mathsf{L}_{2}\mathsf{L}_{3}]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathrm{A}[\mathsf{S}_{1}]\mathrm{S}_{2}[\mathsf{T}\mathsf{L}_{2}\mathsf{L}_{3}]\mathrm{D}[\mathsf{S}_{1}]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathrm{a}\mathrm{S}_{2}[\mathsf{T}\mathsf{L}_{2}\mathsf{L}_{3}]\mathrm{D}[\mathsf{S}_{1}]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathrm{a}\mathrm{S}_{2}[\mathsf{L}_{1}\mathsf{T}\mathsf{L}_{2}\mathsf{L}_{2}\mathsf{L}_{3}]\mathrm{D}[\mathsf{S}_{1}]

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{*}\mathrm{a}\mathrm{a}\mathrm{S}_{2}[\mathsf{L}_{2}\mathsf{L}_{2}\mathsf{L}_{3}]\mathrm{D}[\mathsf{S}_{1}]\mathrm{D}[\mathsf{S}_{1}]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathrm{a}\mathrm{a}\mathrm{B}[\mathsf{S}_{1}]\mathrm{S}_{2}[\mathsf{L}_{2}\mathsf{L}_{3}]\mathrm{C}[\mathsf{S}_{1}]\mathrm{D}[\mathsf{S}_{1}]\mathrm{D}[\mathsf{S}_{1}]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathrm{a}\mathrm{a}\mathrm{b}\mathrm{S}_{2}[\mathsf{L}_{2}\mathsf{L}_{3}]\mathrm{C}[\mathsf{S}_{1}]\mathrm{D}[\mathsf{S}_{1}]\mathrm{D}[\mathsf{S}_{1}]

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{*}\mathrm{a}\mathrm{a}\mathrm{b}\mathrm{b}\mathrm{c}\mathrm{c}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{d}.

Analogous definitions for when the controller is a WPDA and/or the controllee is a WLD-PDA are given in
section

B.1
.

3.2
Normal Form

In this section, we define a normal form for CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG that will help us design fast stringsum and allsum algorithms.
Interestingly, this normal form arises naturally from the normal forms of the controllee and the controller.
Analogous normal forms are derived for PDA
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG, CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA, and PDA
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA in
section

B.2.3
.

Definition 9
.

A WCFG is in
Chomsky normal form
if all of its productions are of one of the following types: (1)
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{S}\rightarrow\varepsilon}
, (2)
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}\rightarrow\mathit{a}}
, or (3)
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}\rightarrow\mathit{Y}\mathit{Z}}
, where
\mathit{S},\mathit{X},\mathit{Y},\mathit{Z}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}
and
\mathit{a}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}
. Moreover,
\mathit{S}
does not appear on the right-hand side of any production.

A WLD-CFG is in Chomsky normal form if all of its productions are of type (1) or (2) above, (3a)
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{389719}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{389719}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{317782}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{194859}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}\mathit{Z}}
, or (3b)
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}\rightarrow\mathit{Y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{321562}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{321562}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{264477}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{160780}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}}
.

Proposition 1
.

For any WCFG
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}
with weights in an
\omega
-continuous semiring, there is a WCFG in Chomsky normal form that defines the same weighted language as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}
.

For any WLD-CFG
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{2}
with weights in an
\omega
-continuous semiring, there is a WLD-CFG in Chomsky normal form that is equivalent to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{2}
.

Proof.

See
section

B.2.1
.
∎

\begin{array}[]{r@{}l@{\qquad}r@{}l@{\qquad}r@{}l@{\qquad}c}\lx@intercol\hfil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{2}\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{2}\hfil\lx@intercol&\text{name}\\
\hline\cr{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\xrightarrow{w_{1}}\ell}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell\colon\mathit{S}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\xrightarrow{w_{2}}\varepsilon}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{S}[\mathsfit S]}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\xrightarrow{w_{1}\otimes w_{2}}\varepsilon}&\text{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}epsilon})}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\xrightarrow{w_{1}}\ell}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell\colon\mathit{X}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\xrightarrow{w_{2}}\mathit{a}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}[\mathsfit A]}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\xrightarrow{w_{1}\otimes w_{2}}\mathit{a}}&\text{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}terminal})}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\xrightarrow{w_{1}}\ell}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell\colon\mathit{X}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\xrightarrow{w_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{389719}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{389719}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{317782}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{194859}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}\mathit{Z}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}[\mathsfit A\mathinner{\cdot\cdot}]}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\xrightarrow{w_{1}\otimes w_{2}}\mathit{Y}[\mathinner{\cdot\cdot}]\mathit{Z}[\mathsfit S]}&\text{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}left pop})}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\xrightarrow{w_{1}}\ell}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell\colon\mathit{X}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\xrightarrow{w_{2}}\mathit{Y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{321562}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{321562}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{264477}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{160780}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}[\mathsfit A\mathinner{\cdot\cdot}]}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\xrightarrow{w_{1}\otimes w_{2}}\mathit{Y}[\mathsfit S]\mathit{Z}[\mathinner{\cdot\cdot}]}&\text{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}right pop})}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\xrightarrow{w_{1}}\mathsfit B\mathsfit C}&&&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}[\mathsfit A\mathinner{\cdot\cdot}]}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\xrightarrow{w_{1}}\mathit{X}[\mathsfit B\mathsfit C\mathinner{\cdot\cdot}]}&\text{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}push})}\end{array}

Figure 2:
Normal form of CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG. Each row shows a rule from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}
(in Chomsky normal form) and possibly a rule from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{2}
(in Chomsky normal form) and the resulting normal-form rule of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{2}
along with its name.

Recall that a CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG is defined as a LD-CFG whose derivations are controlled by another CFG.
When we consider only W(LD-)CFGs in Chomsky normal form, we obtain, for free, a normal form for CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG only by mixing their rules, as shown in
fig.

2
. For the
epsilon
rule, in principle the left-hand side could be
\mathit{S}[\mathsfit A]
for any
\mathsfit A
, but such rules would never be used.

Due to d-strong equivalence,
this normal form also induces a normal form for TAG.
But, in order to convert a TAG into the normal form, one needs to first extract the controller and the controllee as shown by
Butoi et al. 2023
, then convert these to the Chomsky normal form, merge their rules, and convert them back to a TAG, also shown by
Butoi et al. 2023
(see
appendix

E
for a complexity analysis of these transformations).
Although these transformations add some extra complexity, the conversion into the normal form becomes simpler as it only requires (LD-)CFG conversions, rather than a direct TAG conversion.
We leave a direct conversion for future work.

4
Computing Stringsums

In this section, we give a deduction system for computing stringsums of a particular string
\boldsymbol{s}
in CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG in normal form.
Stringsums of a string
\boldsymbol{s}
can be computed analogously in PDA
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG, CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA, and PDA
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA; we show the deduction systems for these in
section

C.2
.
Due to d-strong equivalence, these deduction systems can also be used for spinal TAG, LIG, EPDA, and spinal PAA by first converting them to their equivalent two-level formalism.
Our algorithms have improved space requirements and run asymptotically faster than the best algorithms for LIG and EPDA.
Moreover, it is the only stringsum algorithm for PAA that we are aware of.

4.1
Pop Computations

In order to compute stringsums efficiently, we decompose derivations into shareable smaller parts. Our decomposition is based on a concept that has been extensively used in the literature
Kuhlmann et al. 2011
;
Butoi et al. 2022
for decomposing runs of pushdown automata, namely pop computations.
In the context of WPDAs, a pop computation is a partial derivation that has the overall effect of popping a single stack symbol, leaving the rest of the stack unchanged
Butoi et al. 2022
.
We can define pop computations of CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG with a similar interpretation: partial derivations that “pop” a single leftmost nonterminal symbol, leaving the rest of the nonterminals unchanged.
We define pop computations of CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG formally below.
The pop computations of PDA
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG, CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA, and PDA
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA are defined similarly; we present these in
section

C.1
.

Definition 10
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}
be a controller WCFG and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{2}
be a WLD-CFG, with nonterminal alphabets
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}_{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}_{2}
, respectively.
Let
\boldsymbol{s}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}^{*}}
be a string of length
|\boldsymbol{s}|=n
. A
pop computation
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{G}}_{2}
of type
\mathit{X}[\mathsfit{A}\,\mathinner{\cdot\cdot}]
i
l
j
k
\mathit{Y}[\mathinner{\cdot\cdot}]
, where
0\leq i\leq j\leq k\leq l\leq n
,
\mathit{X},\mathit{Y}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}_{2}
, and
\mathsfit A\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}_{1}
, is a pair of partial derivations
(d_{1},d_{2})
, where

\displaystyle d_{1}

\displaystyle\in({\color[rgb]{0.6406,0.0273,0.4531}\mathit{X}[\mathsfit A]}\xRightarrow{*}{\color[rgb]{0.6406,0.0273,0.4531}\boldsymbol{s}_{(i\mathord{:}j]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4688,0.582}\mathit{Y}[\varepsilon]}{\color[rgb]{0.6406,0.0273,0.4531}\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{2}})

\displaystyle d_{2}

\displaystyle\in({\color[rgb]{0.6406,0.0273,0.4531}\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{2}}\xRightarrow{*}{\color[rgb]{0.6406,0.0273,0.4531}\boldsymbol{s}_{(k\mathord{:}l]}}).

The weight of the pop computation is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{w}}(d_{1},d_{2})={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{w}}(d_{1})\otimes{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{w}}(d_{2})
.

A
pop computation
of type
\mathit{X}[\mathsfit{A}]
i
j
is a partial derivation
{\color[rgb]{0.6406,0.0273,0.4531}\mathit{X}[\mathsfit A]}\xRightarrow{*}{\color[rgb]{0.6406,0.0273,0.4531}\boldsymbol{s}_{(i\mathord{:}j]}}
.

In words, a pop computation of a CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG pops a single controller symbol and derives a string possibly with a
gap
.
fig.

3
shows a visual representation of a pop computation of a CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG.

\mathit{X}[\mathsfit{A}\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
\boldsymbol{s}_{i}
\boldsymbol{s}_{j}
\boldsymbol{s}_{k}
\boldsymbol{s}_{l}
\mathit{Y}[\boldsymbol{\alpha}]

Figure 3:
A pop computation of a CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG is a partial derivation possibly with a gap (the teal part). First,
{\color[rgb]{0.6406,0.0273,0.4531}\mathit{X}[\mathsfit A\boldsymbol{\alpha}]}
derives
{\color[rgb]{0.6406,0.0273,0.4531}\boldsymbol{s}_{(i\mathord{:}k]}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6406,0.0273,0.4531}\mathit{Y}[\boldsymbol{\alpha}]}
, popping one nonterminal
\mathsfit A
. Then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4688,0.582}\mathit{Y}[\boldsymbol{\alpha}]}
derives the gap (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4688,0.582}\boldsymbol{s}_{(k\mathord{:}l]}}
). Finally, the purple part of the partial derivation resumes, deriving
{\color[rgb]{0.6406,0.0273,0.4531}\boldsymbol{s}_{(l\mathord{:}j]}}
. The weight of the pop computation is the weight of the purple part of the partial derivation.

4.2
Deduction System

We compute stringsums of CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG efficiently by exploiting the decomposable structure of the pop computations.
We give the stringsum algorithm as a weighted deduction system
(
Goodman 1999
)
, shown in
fig.

4
a.

Items.

The deductive system has two types of items, which correspond to the two types of pop computations (one with a gap and one without a gap). The weights of these items are the total weights of the pop computations of those types.
